        As people have different sexual preferences, the sex robot industry is likely to produce diverse models, mirroring the adult entertainment industry [368]. This may reduce social pressure to adhere to beauty standards.

        1.4.5.2.2. Con:
        People with non-ideal body types may be considered non-ideal partners, regardless of the existence of sex robots.

          1.4.5.2.2.1. Pro:
          Women in sex work often conform [369] to stereotypical ideas of attractiveness and may contribute to men's unrealistic expectations of women and their bodies.

        1.4.5.2.3. Pro:
        Sex robots have similar bodies to porn stars, promoting an idealised and often unrealistic beauty standard [370].

        1.4.5.2.4. Pro:
        In addition to feminine features, sex robots can have characteristics that are impossible [371] for humans to emulate. Women may struggle to compete with these sex robots and internalise a sense of inferiority.

      1.4.5.3. Con:
      Sex robots of other genders are also depicted using idealised versions of human bodies - there is no reason to believe these are less harmful than depictions of women.

        1.4.5.3.1. Con:
        That sex robots perpetuate oppression against more people than just women is not a reason to believe they are liberatory, rather it is a cause for further concern about their role in society.

        1.4.5.3.2. Pro:
        Masculinised sex robots [372] are as customisable [373] as their feminine counterparts; this is not a gendered issue, but rather a case of consumers creating their idyll, regardless of social propriety.

      1.4.5.4. Con:
      Sex robots may be less objectifying and reductive than sex toys that emulate disembodied vaginas or penises. This is a step towards moving beyond objectification.

        1.4.5.4.1. Pro:
        Sex toys that consist solely of emulated genitals support the notion that a person can be reduced to a single part and thus contribute to the objectification of humans (p. 2 [374]).

        1.4.5.4.2. Con:
        Sex robots may have genitalia that look and feel realistic, yet their bodies represent unrealistic beauty standards that often objectify women. As the only realistic features of sex robots may be their genitalia, this is not a step forward.

    1.4.6. Con:
    Since sex robots are a product of culture rather than an actor that contributes to it, they have no cultural agency. Thus, they cannot further misogyny, only evidence it.

      1.4.6.1. Pro:
      As sexual attraction to women is separate from misogyny, having sex with a feminised robot is unlikely to induce hatred towards women or lead users to support their oppression.

        1.4.6.1.1. Con:
        Incels often express unfulfilled sexual desires [375] that lead them to despise [376] the women that reject them. As such, misogyny contributes to, and is influenced by, sexual attraction to women.

        1.4.6.1.2. Pro:
        Solidarity movements, such as the HeForShe [377] campaign, have successfully demonstrated that men and boys, many of whom are sexually attracted to women, can remain committed to promoting gender equality and discouraging misogynistic behaviour.

        1.4.6.1.3. Pro:
        One need not feel hatred or contempt for women in order to be sexually attracted to them. Many women want to be sexually desirable [378] and consider sexual attraction to be an important facet of romantic relationships.

        1.4.6.1.4. Con:
        Feminised sex robots that cannot communicate consent may affirm the sexist idea that women are passive and ever-consenting to sex, furthering misogyny.

          1.4.6.1.4.1. Pro:
          Sex robots are not required to consent, as they are designed for the sole pleasure of the user. When in a feminine form and used by men, this could perpetuate ideas of female subservience and a misogynistic disregard for consent.

            1.4.6.1.4.1.1. Pro:
            Harmony sex dolls [379] are docile, fulfilling patriarchal ideals of female submission [380] in ways that human women may be reluctant to.

            1.4.6.1.4.1.2. Con:
            Differing expectations of men and women about appropriate sexual frequency may be based on the physiological effects of higher testosterone levels [381] in men as opposed to learned factors.

          1.4.6.1.4.2. Con:
          Even if sex robots are feminised, men will be able to tell the difference between robots and women (p. 1 [382]). This may create a cognitive distinction that distinguishes the treatment of women versus sex robots.

          1.4.6.1.4.3. Pro:
          Many women already feel a decided lack of autonomy [383] in their sexual relationships; female sex robots may further reinforce this belief.

      1.4.6.2. Con:
      People can adopt and perpetuate others' misogynistic or sexist views through the work they consume and produce. Thus, agency is not required for something to be a vehicle for harm.

        1.4.6.2.1. Con:
        The misogyny evidenced by sex robots is likely to rapidly diminish as women become more involved in and catered to by the sex toy industry.

          1.4.6.2.1.1. Pro:
          Women's experiences in the sex industry are considerably more empowering [384] now than historically. This in turn may impact the sex robot market, making them more egalitarian.

          1.4.6.2.1.2. Con:
          The increased marketisation of women's pleasure is not inherently liberatory, particularly as it leverages the medicalisation of sex [385] to fuel demand.

          1.4.6.2.1.3. Pro:
          Sinthetics, a major sex doll company, claim that their orders for masculinised dolls are equal [386] to feminised ones.

        1.4.6.2.2. Pro:
        Car safety systems such as seat belts are designed for male bodies, resulting in an almost 50% higher risk of serious injury for female drivers (p. 2368 [387]). This demonstrates that inanimate objects can further sexism.

        1.4.6.2.3. Pro:
        People, systems, or organisations can be sexist unintentionally [388], causing harm through implicit bias [389]. As such, sex robots may be sexist despite having no agency.

      1.4.6.3. Con:
      Sex toys for men, a category that sex robots frequently fall into (p. 4 [390]), may be marketed in a way that diminishes or objectifies [391] women and perpetuates such attitudes and behaviours in users.

        1.4.6.3.1. Con:
        The objectification and sexualisation of women is omnipresent in advertising (p. 5 [392]); this is a broader social issue that transcends the sex toy market.

        1.4.6.3.2. Con:
        The marketing of sex toys reflects, rather than creates, sexual stereotypes in order to drive sales by aligning with consumer attitudes and beliefs.

        1.4.6.3.3. Pro:
        Online advertising for the Silicone Stroker sex toy have included depictions of sexual violence and body-shaming [393].

        1.4.6.3.4. Pro:
        Whizworx were heavily criticised [394] for an advertisement that body-shamed women in order to sell a male masturbatory aid.

      1.4.6.4. Pro:
      Sex robots are not human beings, and thus can no more contribute to societal misogyny than toasters.

        1.4.6.4.1. Pro:
        Sex robots, like other technologies, are inherently neutral [395]. If sex robots are used in ways that promote misogyny, this is attributable to poor choices made by humans.

        1.4.6.4.2. Con:
        Toasters are not gendered, do not closely imitate a humanoid form, and are not designed to mimic intimate interpersonal behaviour, unlike sex robots. It is these characteristics of sex robots that enable them to contribute to social attitudes about sex and gender.

  1.5. Con:
  Sex robots are unlikely to ever be a mainstream technology.

    1.5.1. Con:
    The sexual liberation movement is solely concerned with the freedom of individuals. As such, sex robots do not need to be mainstream in order to help advance it.

      1.5.1.1. Con:
      Sexual liberation is a collective endeavor [396]; the structural changes needed for sex robots to promote liberation will not be achieved unless their use is common.

        1.5.1.1.1. Pro:
        In order for sex robots to make a meaningful contribution to sexual liberation, a substantial number of sex robot users must understand the systematic implications of their individual experiences and work together to promote change, as was the case for second-wave [397] feminists.

        1.5.1.1.2. Con:
        Widespread is not the same as mainstream. Even if sex robots are not mainstream in the sense that their use is normalised, there may still be a sufficient number of users to achieve sexual liberation.

      1.5.1.2. Pro:
      Sexual liberation, as characterised by the sexual revolution [398] of the 1960s and 1970s, is associated with a shift [399] towards individual fulfillment.

        1.5.1.2.1. Pro:
        By promoting the personal sexual choices of individuals like previous liberatory sex movements (p. 2 [400]), sex robots inherently contribute to sexual liberation.

        1.5.1.2.2. Con:
        Sex robots are likely to promote existing sexual values (p. 30 [401]) instead of sexual individuality in order to drive widespread sales.

      1.5.1.3. Pro:
      Sexual liberation is defined [402] as the loss of an individual's sexual inhibitions. Sex robots may facilitate greater freedoms and, therefore, advance sexual liberation.

        1.5.1.3.1. Pro:
        Sex robot technology will create a new type of social interaction in the form of intimate relationships with increasingly advanced AI systems. This type of interaction has been found to be highly disinhibiting [403].

        1.5.1.3.2. Con:
        Although users may feel a lack of inhibition with sex robots, it may be attributable to a lack of human judgement. As it is not certain that such disinhibition will carry over into human relationships, sexual liberation is unlikely to be advanced.

      1.5.1.4. Con:
      Sexual liberation is a social process [404]. Therefore, sex robots need to be mainstream in order to create any positive effect.

        1.5.1.4.1. Pro:
        An examination of social change over the past 50 years shows that changes to the foundational norms of a society, such as sexual norms, require substantial support and popularity [405], instead of being progressed by singular events.

        1.5.1.4.2. Con:
        Some research suggests that successfully engaging 3.5% of a population [406] is sufficient for enacting social change.

    1.5.2. Con:
    Almost all technological developments have experienced mass adoption [407] shortly after they become known to the public, usually correlating with affordable commercial release.

      1.5.2.1. Pro:
      Some believe that sex with robots will be widespread by 2050 [408], and will surpass sexual interaction between humans.

        1.5.2.1.1. Pro:
        Indicators suggest that around 39% of the population [409] would already be willing to try sex robots. This may make future widespread sex robot adoption more likely.

        1.5.2.1.2. Con:
        Even if sex robots are commonplace, it is likely that they will be an object of ridicule, and treated as a forebear of the decline of society [410].

        1.5.2.1.3. Pro:
        Sex robots have been conceptualised [411] since the earliest days of humanity, meaning that current advancements in technology are a realisation of a long-held aspiration for humanity and so may be lauded.

          1.5.2.1.3.1. Con:
          In some areas of the world, sex dolls may not cohere with myth; the Japanese ethnic religion of Shinto [412] has a complicated relationship with dolls, in that they are humanoid, and are treated as having a soul [413].

          1.5.2.1.3.2. Pro:
          In Greek mythology, the god Hephaestus [414] created helpers and handmaidens out of gold to compensate for his lameness, and provide companionship.

          1.5.2.1.3.3. Pro:
          Jewish and Norse legends feature golems [415], which are inanimate clay or mud objects that gain sentience. These have a wide variety of both positive and negative connotations.

      1.5.2.2. Pro:
      Virtual reality [416] was presumed to remain niche, yet it is inexorably growing in popularity. Sex robots may follow a similar trajectory.

        1.5.2.2.1. Con:
        Millions of dollars of capital have been invested [417] in VR companies, indicating that investors highly value its future. No such quantities of money have been given over to sex robot companies.

        1.5.2.2.2. Pro:
        Sex robot creators are interested in how virtual reality [418] could interact with artificial intelligence; the end goal would allow users to "interact with virtual avatars in an actual space". This utilisation of existing technology bodes well for future developments.

      1.5.2.3. Con:
      There are issues with how the artificial intelligence in sex robots will operate, given that it interfaces with app software [419]; this will require the consent and collaboration of app providers.

        1.5.2.3.1. Pro:
        The Harmony sex robot app is not currently available [420] on Apple products due to adult content restrictions [421]. Such restrictions are likely to limit the mass adoption of sex robots.

        1.5.2.3.2. Con:
        Technology invariably provokes social development [422]. As sexuality and technology develop in tandem, sex robots may become more widespread and acceptable, such that tech businesses are more amenable to the technology.

          1.5.2.3.2.1. Pro:
          Intimate relationships between humans and robots are commonly portrayed in anime and science fiction, normalising sex robots while also signalling their links to social development and futurism.

            1.5.2.3.2.1.1. Pro:
            Popular movies and shows, such as Westworld [423] and Blade Runner [424], have focused on relationships between humans and robots.

            1.5.2.3.2.1.2. Con:
            There are public misconceptions surrounding many aspects of technological development; science fiction has imbued many with incorrect perceptions [425] of current artificial intelligence, for example.

          1.5.2.3.2.2. Con:
          Some research [426] has suggested that technology might also stunt social and emotional development, and thus be detrimental to society.

      1.5.2.4. Con:
      Existing high-tech masturbation technologies are not widespread, indicating that there is little demand for sex robots.

        1.5.2.4.1. Con:
        Sex robots are likely to include a wealth of useful and desirable technology [427], such as home security features and bonding technology that allows a high degree of personalisation. This will contribute to their adoption.

        1.5.2.4.2. Pro:
        The mostly highly recommended sex toys [428] are often simple vibrators and dildos; high-tech toys are likely to remain niche.

    1.5.3. Pro:
    There is a stigma [429] around using sex toys which may prevent sex robots from becoming mainstream.

      1.5.3.1. Con:
      Sex toys have long been normalised for women, and while men have been stigmatised [430] in this market, sex robots will be help to balance this.

        1.5.3.1.1. Pro:
        Men who use sex robots have begun to organize discussion groups [431] about them, just as women organize parties to discuss and sample sex toys [432]. This will help remove the stigma attached to sex robot use.

        1.5.3.1.2. Con:
        Stigma about men using sex toys is based on entrenched sexual norms and values [433] rather than on the use of toys itself, and is therefore unlikely to be changed by the introduction of sex robots.

      1.5.3.2. Con:
      Masturbation is now less taboo than it once was, as it is increasingly portrayed in TV and film [434].

        1.5.3.2.1. Pro:
        Since the 1995 firing of US Surgeon General Dr. Jocelyn Elders for promoting masturbation education for young people, many people around the world have continued to celebrate May as National Masturbation Month [435], promoting awareness and education and helping to decrease stigma.

        1.5.3.2.2. Con:
        Female masturbation is still stigmatised and is far less commonly discussed [436] or shown in media.

      1.5.3.3. Pro:
      The stigma surrounding sex toy use can lead to widespread concerns about privacy and data breaches in app-controlled toys. Were sex robots to face—like We-Vibe [437] sex toys—a major privacy scandal, their popularity could greatly decline.

        1.5.3.3.1. Con:
        Security concerns are unlikely to pose a substantial barrier to usage; despite the associated privacy and security risks, more than half of the global population [438] is already using the internet.

        1.5.3.3.2. Pro:
        Users are likely to regard any information related to their sexual habits as highly confidential; breaches of such data could contribute to technology-assisted sexual violence [439].

      1.5.3.4. Pro:
      Many religions would consider the use of sex robots immoral and seek to limit their followers' engagement with the technology.

        1.5.3.4.1. Con:
        Pope Francis advanced Catholic doctrine by saying that premarital sex is not a serious sin [440]. As sexual standards and faith shift, sex robots may be permitted.

          1.5.3.4.1.1. Con:
          Masturbation [441] is believed to be morally wrong in Catholicism, so sex robots are likely to be considered in the same way.

          1.5.3.4.1.2. Con:
          Pope Paul VI, the highest authority figure of the Catholic Church, reaffirmed [442] the necessity of marital acts to be linked to procreation. Ergo, sex robots are utterly prohibited.

          1.5.3.4.1.3. Pro:
          The existing Catholic guidance would allow the use of sex robots to aid women in conceiving, in a similar fashion to PopDildo [443] products.

        1.5.3.4.2. Pro:
        Some Muslim scholars believe that sex robots are incompatable with an ethical Muslim life and could carry heavy penalties for those that use them (p. 21 [444]).

        1.5.3.4.3. Con:
        Sex robots might satisfy the sexual desires of those who practise – for example, on religious grounds – abstinence before marriage.

          1.5.3.4.3.1. Con:
          While some teens cite religious belief as a reason for abstaining from sex, many have other reasons [445] for choosing to do so.

          1.5.3.4.3.2. Pro:
          No Bible verse condemns the use of objects for sexual pleasure. Thus, there is no biblical reason to justify prohibiting the use of sex robots.

            1.5.3.4.3.2.1. Pro:
            Focus on the Family, a deeply conservative [446] organisation, have concluded that, because the Bible is silent on the issue of sex toys, each couple must draw their own conclusions [447] about their acceptability.

            1.5.3.4.3.2.2. Con:
            Within Christianity, sex is intended to strengthen the bond between a man and woman. As sex toys emphasise bodily pleasure [448] rather than intimacy, their use can be unbiblical.

          1.5.3.4.3.3. Con:
          Abstinence [449] for cultural or religious reasons can exclude all forms of sexual interaction; sex robots are unlikely to be used by such consumers.

        1.5.3.4.4. Pro:
        Given that around 84% of the world [450] is religious in some way or form, it is vital to understand how different groups are likely to respond as the technology develops. If religious groups shun sex robots, they are unlikely to advance sexual liberation.

        1.5.3.4.5. Pro:
        Some believe that replacing human relationships with machines is a form of idolatry [451]; rather than participating in human sex, such individuals are paying exclusive attention to a simulacrum, which is immoral.

        1.5.3.4.6. Con:
        As morality is relative [452], and there is no conclusive evidence that any given religion or belief system is true, morality cannot be defined by religious doctrine.

          1.5.3.4.6.1. Con:
          Ideas of moral relativism presuppose a greater diversity amongst cultures than what is actually found. Many religions and cultures display significant overlap [453] in their moral principles.

            1.5.3.4.6.1.1. Pro:
            A comparison of multiple cultures reveals invariant development of moral judgement based upon the principle of justice (p. 10 [454]).

            1.5.3.4.6.1.2. Con:
            There is immense cultural diversity [455] in the development and application of morality. This may mean that some cultures find sex robots to be moral, while others decry them.

          1.5.3.4.6.2. Con:
          As society makes meta judgments about its own morality, there must be objective moral standards, such as those embodied in religious doctrine, that can also be used to judge sex robots.

            1.5.3.4.6.2.1. Con:
            Research on the function of the human brain whilst making moral judgments shows great divergence between types of judgments, indicating a lack of unifying moral principles (p. 3171 [456]).

            1.5.3.4.6.2.2. Pro:
            The idea of social progress is predicated on the existence of independent values which can be better achieved (p. 21 [457]). People should strive to ascertain these to assess the moral value of sex robots.

          1.5.3.4.6.3. Pro:
          The morality of sex [458] is not distinct from the morality of other acts; using a robot for sex is the moral equivalent of using a robot for any other purpose.

            1.5.3.4.6.3.1. Pro:
            Sex is an entirely physical function to which humans often ascribe personal and cultural meanings that are in no way inherent to the act (p. 2 [459]).

            1.5.3.4.6.3.2. Pro:
            There is no reason to attribute sex acts a special moral status [460]. If it is ethical to use robots for any purpose, such as to serve food, then it is also ethical to use them for sex.

            1.5.3.4.6.3.3. Con:
            Sex with robots is divergent from the natural form of human sexual desire, which is oriented towards other humans. In this way, it can be considered an immoral perversion [461] that impedes sexual liberation.

            1.5.3.4.6.3.4. Con:
            As an act inherently focused on the bodies of other human beings, sex should be attributed a unique moral status [462].

          1.5.3.4.6.4. Pro:
          Sex outside of marriage, casual sex, and promiscuity are now more common and accepted than they have been previously [463]. Changes in sexual mores demonstrates the relative nature of morality and its susceptibility to change separate to religious doctrine.

      1.5.3.5. Pro:
      Societal acceptance of less technologically advanced sex toys is far from universal, as they are considered by some to be immoral. Sex robots are likely to be viewed similarly.

        1.5.3.5.1. Pro:
        Sex toys remain illegal [464] in a number of countries.

        1.5.3.5.2. Pro:
        Chile's economic minister came under fire [465] for accepting a sex doll as a gift.

        1.5.3.5.3. Con:
        The global market for sex toys is worth over US$30 billion [466] per annum and continues to grow. Such liberalization may also benefit the sex robots market.

    1.5.4. Pro:
    Sex robots are likely to be incredibly expensive, inhibiting their widespread adoption.

      1.5.4.1. Con:
      Technology often rapidly decreases [467] in cost as companies innovate new cost-saving measures.

        1.5.4.1.1. Pro:
        3D printing may make sex robots significantly cheaper [468], allowing more consumers to access them than ever before.

        1.5.4.1.2. Con:
        Cheaper sex robots may have limited features [469] compared to more expensive models.

      1.5.4.2. Pro:
      -> See 1.4.4.3.2.

      1.5.4.3. Con:
      There is a chance of second-hand robots being sold at a more reasonable price.

        1.5.4.3.1. Con:
        People are unlikely to want to rent, share, or buy a sex robot second hand. This could further drive up their price.

          1.5.4.3.1.1. Pro:
          There are health and sanitation concerns [470] associated with second-hand sex toys that would also apply to sex robots.

          1.5.4.3.1.2. Con:
          Given that sex robot brothels already exist [471], this is clearly not entirely distasteful to users.

        1.5.4.3.2. Pro:
        There has been a recent increase in the sales of second-hand sex toys [472]; people may also become amenable to the idea of used sex robots as they become more common.

      1.5.4.4. Pro:
      The high cost of sex robots may decrease their accessibility to the people they could help the most, such as older people [473] or people with disabilities [474], who tend to be less wealthy.

        1.5.4.4.1. Pro:
        Both older people [475] and those living with disabilities [476] are far more likely to be socially isolated compared with other groups, and could, thus, benefit greatly from the companionship offered by sex robots.

        1.5.4.4.2. Con:
        If sex robots are demonstrated to improve the health of older or disabled users, then insurance and subsidies [477] may help to cover their cost in part or in full.

    1.5.5. Pro:
    Sex robots have numerous problems: they are bulky, hard to store, require cleaning, etc.

      1.5.5.1. Con:
      Sex with robots could be more convenient than that with human partners, as it requires little to no time investment.

        1.5.5.1.1. Pro:
        -> See 1.4.4.3.1.

        1.5.5.1.2. Con:
        Widespread use of sex robots may reduce people's expectations about time investment in intimate human relationships.

          1.5.5.1.2.1. Pro:
          Reducing people's expectations of how much time they invest in intimate relationships could work against sexual liberation as it supports a low-investment male reproductive strategy while undermining the strategy traditionally used by women (pp. 63, 66 [478]).

          1.5.5.1.2.2. Con:
          Reducing women's time investment in dating might advance sexual liberation, since there is currently a notable disparity between men and women in this category (p. 125 [479]).

      1.5.5.2. Pro:
      The weight of current sex robots can be up to 105lbs [480], which is quite difficult for a lot of people to easily manoeuvre.

        1.5.5.2.1. Con:
        The weight of sex robots may come down significantly through the use of commonly available light-weight materials. Such refinements are common [481] in technological development.

        1.5.5.2.2. Con:
        Weight may not be a major factor in sex robot adoption since they are likely to predominantly be used in a home setting.

        1.5.5.2.3. Pro:
        The size of sex robots may be prohibitive to their adoption given the necessity of keeping them hidden as intimate personal items. Other sex toys are considerably less conspicuous.

        1.5.5.2.4. Pro:
        Men may feel uncomfortable with sex robots if they are large [482], heavy [483], and potentially re-programmable for genital-based violence.

      1.5.5.3. Con:
      Sex robots could eventually serve multiple purposes like helping with household chores. If they were accepted in such a capacity, then they would likely be more widely adopted.

        1.5.5.3.1. Con:
        Given the social stigma around openly displaying sexual paraphernalia, it is unlikely that people will be comfortable with having their masturbatory aid contribute to household chores.

        1.5.5.3.2. Con:
        Creating a general-purpose mechanical assistant requires levels of artificial intelligence [484] and animatronics that will still be far off after decades of research.

        1.5.5.3.3. Pro:
        Many sex toys have a nominal application in a non-sexual context [485] while many normal appliances also can be used as a sexual aid.

          1.5.5.3.3.1. Pro:
          Vibrators first achieved prominence as a home appliance [486] designed for non-sexual massage and were pseudoscientifically touted as the cure for a number of medical conditions.

        1.5.5.3.4. Pro:
        Sex robot technology is continually being honed and developed such that the ability to use them in the house is already feasible [487].

      1.5.5.4. Pro:
      Sex robots could be reprogrammed or hacked to inflict harm on their users, or even used to kill [488]. These security issues are likely to deter widespread adoption.

        1.5.5.4.1. Con:
        Sex robots could be programmed to consider consequential ethics [489], not only duty ethics. This may make them harder to hack for malicious purposes.

        1.5.5.4.2. Pro:
        Reports from digital security firms indicate that it is far easier for cyber attacks [490] to breach industrial robot security than is commonly assumed. Sex robots may suffer from similar vulnerabilities.

        1.5.5.4.3. Pro:
        Sex robots may be susceptible to ransomware attacks [491], which could be especially damaging to users who have developed an emotional attachment to their robot.

        1.5.5.4.4. Con:
        Hacked sex robots would not constitute a greater threat to users than humans [492].

          1.5.5.4.4.1. Pro:
          Humans have a genetic predisposition towards violence [493] that sex robots do not have.

          1.5.5.4.4.2. Con:
          The potential physical strength of mechanical robot components far outstrips [494] the strength of a human and makes assault by robots a greater threat.

  1.6. Pro:
  Sex robots could allow people to take on more ethical and liberating relationship arrangements.

    1.6.1. Pro:
    Sex robots could help focus relationships on emotional bonding instead of sexual gratification.

      1.6.1.1. Pro:
      Some people may choose to form artificial families [495] with their robot companions, including artificial children [496]; this would theoretically involve the merging of characteristics from the human and robot to create the brain and body, and 'giving birth' via 3D printing.

        1.6.1.1.1. Pro:
        One sex robot has a family setting [497], which enables it to discuss a range of topics; this could enrich family life.

        1.6.1.1.2. Con:
        Technologically speaking, cyborgs [498] are a very long way off, and robotic children who can feasibly grow and develop are equally distant.

        1.6.1.1.3. Con:
        Having a sex robot as part of a family unit raises many questions [499] about appropriateness and the potential repercussions on children who grow up in the presence of a humanoid robot.

          1.6.1.1.3.1. Pro:
          Many parents would be averse [500] to their children interacting with their sex toys, regardless of how advanced the toys might be.

          1.6.1.1.3.2. Pro:
          Children—when allowed free reign with a robot—ended up beating it to pieces, showing 'frightening moral principles [501].'

            1.6.1.1.3.2.1. Pro:
            Three quarters of abusive children perceived the robot they destroyed as humanoid and decided to abuse it regardless; many cited enjoyment as their motivation for doing so (p. 2 [502]).

          1.6.1.1.3.3. Con:
          As technology is developing, ever more studies are emerging to understand the psychology behind how children and robots interact (p. 2 [503]). By the time sex robots are marketable norms, we will be considerably further in understanding how to approach this question.

            1.6.1.1.3.3.1. Con:
            There is rising evidence to suggest that children who grow up in an environment with both humans and humanoid robots would be extremely confused [504] and could develop psychoses as a result of the disjunct between their expectations and the behavior of humanoid robots.

        1.6.1.1.4. Pro:
        There is a potential market for sex robots as a reproductive aid, if they integrate technology like some other sex toys [505].

      1.6.1.2. Con:
      To the extent that some human relationships are driven by a desire for sexual activity, the adoption of sex robots could lead to the formation of fewer relationships.

        1.6.1.2.1. Pro:
        Sexual gratification is a key element in the development of romantic attachment between humans (p. 464 [506]). Removing this element from human interaction will reduce the formation of emotional bonds.

        1.6.1.2.2. Con:
        Sexual desire is only one of the motivations [507] for humans seeking romantic relationships.

      1.6.1.3. Pro:
      Long-term monogamous relationships may go through periods where one partner is less interested in sex than the other. Sex robots may be an option to ensure both partners remain satisfied.

        1.6.1.3.1. Pro:
        Women whose husbands own sex robots report [508] that they have positive experiences [509], and do not feel replaced or undermined. Normalising these robots therefore may allow people to form more stable families and improve their lives.

        1.6.1.3.2. Con:
        Some families report intense discord [510] because of their relationships with sex robots. It is unlikely that people will have uniformly positive responses, so families may actually be harmed.

        1.6.1.3.3. Pro:
        Desire discrepancy [511], which can cause severe tension in relationships, is a common problem experienced by couples. Sex robots could offset this and bolster couples who are struggling with it.

          1.6.1.3.3.1. Pro:
          People are often expected to provide sex [512] to their partners. It could be liberating for them to feel less pressure to do so, as sex dolls act as a realistic but non-human alternative.

          1.6.1.3.3.2. Con:
          Using a sex robot to remediate a desire discrepancy could lead to jealousy or insecurity within a relationship, as the less sexually interested party may feel replaced.

          1.6.1.3.3.3. Pro:
          People may not feel comfortable [513] having open relationships, as they can lead to feelings of jealousy. However, partners could use sex robots for sexual satisfaction without emotional complications.

        1.6.1.3.4. Con:
        People in monogamous relationships should devote time to openly discussing, working through, and resolving difficult issues about their sex life rather than rely on sex robots for pleasure.

          1.6.1.3.4.1. Con:
          Sex robots—like other toys such as vibrators—could support a couple's healthy sexual dynamic, ensuring that both are satisfied. In many ways, a sex robot is the ultimate unicorn [514].

          1.6.1.3.4.2. Pro:
          Research has shown that open sexual communication between partners is directly related to both sexual satisfaction and overall satisfaction with the relationship (p. 604 [515]).

          1.6.1.3.4.3. Pro:
          Mutual sexual gratification [516] is a key means of creating and reinforcing emotional bonds. To the extent that this role is filled by sex robots, it risks undermining human relationships.

      1.6.1.4. Con:
      The introduction of sex robots could remove one of the motivations for couples to live together (pp. 9-10 [517]), leading to social isolation.

        1.6.1.4.1. Con:
        Couples already do not need to live together [518]. Thus, living together is an active choice that is unlikely to be changed by the introduction of sex robots to a relationship.

        1.6.1.4.2. Con:
        There are a number of reasons why couples choose to live together [519] that would remain unaffected by the introduction of sex robots, for example, the desire to spend more time together, or test their compatability.

        1.6.1.4.3. Pro:
        As society becomes more individualistic [520], people are likely to suffer more [521]. Eroding relationships—which can be a bulwark against this trend—is likely to be highly detrimental.

    1.6.2. Con:
    The risk of unidirectional emotional bonding [522] could make sex robots detrimental to functional reciprocal relationships (p. 1 [523]).

      1.6.2.1. Pro:
      Interacting socially with robots might poison human interactions [524], as social graces or manners are not a primary concern. This may lead to greater social isolation.

        1.6.2.1.1. Con:
        Research suggests that the incidence of incivility and rudeness in human interactions is very high, even without the influence of robot interaction (p. S58 [525]).

          1.6.2.1.1.1. Con:
          Even if increased rudeness is a preexisting trend, interacting more with robots could exacerbate the problem, particularly in intimate settings.

        1.6.2.1.2. Pro:
        Many have identified associations between increased technology use and decreased intimacy in human interactions (p. 2 [526]).

        1.6.2.1.3. Con:
        Researchers at Yale have observed the opposite phenomenon, wherein AI interactions have helped to improve interactions [527] in human groups.

        1.6.2.1.4. Pro:
        There is a risk that sexual relations with robots will "desensitize [528] humans to intimacy and empathy".

          1.6.2.1.4.1. Con:
          Empathy desensitisation [529] has been utilised for therapeutic ends in overcoming obsessive-compulsive disorder, phobias, post-traumatic stress, and other conditions rooted in anxiety. If sex robots enable this, they may help people significantly.

        1.6.2.1.5. Pro:
        70% of respondents believe that sex robots might harm relationships with other humans (p. 254 [530]).

      1.6.2.2. Con:
      Some believe that one-directional affection for a robot is not necessarily a bad thing [531], since many forms of love are unreciprocated.

        1.6.2.2.1. Pro:
        There is a risk of unidirectional bonding in many human relationships as well, including codependent relationships [532] and relationships involving people with attachment disorders [533].

        1.6.2.2.2. Con:
        Studies have identified minimal benefits to the individual from unreciprocated love in relation to the costs they bear (p. 12 [534]). Increasing the frequency of this is likely to be highly negative.

        1.6.2.2.3. Con:
        Levels of human empathy have already been in a substantial decline for decades. Contributing further to this trend could have serious implications [535] for society.

        1.6.2.2.4. Pro:
        It may be harder for people to process unrequited love for another person than for a sex robot. This is because sex robot users are more likely to understand that their feelings cannot be reciprocated, making it easier to move on.

      1.6.2.3. Pro:
      Robot ethicist John Sullins argues that this illusionary bonding "should not be used [536] to fool people in ascribing more feelings to the machine than they should," and that love is deep, nuanced, and can never be satisfied by a robot.

        1.6.2.3.1. Pro:
        Since the primary characteristic of love is "investment in the well-being of the other for his or her own sake" (p. 635 [537]), robots are incapable of achieving this: a robot does not do anything "for its own sake," but rather follows its programming.

        1.6.2.3.2. Con:
        Researchers in AI technology are working to develop digital hormones [538] that mimic the human endocrine system and would allow robots to more accurately experience human emotions.

    1.6.3. Pro:
    Sex robots could allow people who are physically separated [539], or otherwise unable to have sex, to maintain a sexual connection using realistic forms of cyber sex, in which a person could control a robot for their partner.

      1.6.3.1. Con:
      Cybersex has been identified as a highly addictive [540] pursuit that can lead to problematic sexual behaviour in otherwise normal people. Sex robots that encourage this would likely set back sexual liberation.

        1.6.3.1.1. Pro:
        Maintaining a positive and regular sexual connection can contribute to pair-bonding in couples [541], so a sexual substitute would be counterproductive.

        1.6.3.1.2. Con:
        Cybersex encompasses a wide range of behaviours; many of the negative perceptions about cybersex confuse different use cases [542]. For example, cybersex where a couple uses technology to facilitate a long-distance relationship is very different from cybersex with strangers or illegal cybersex.

      1.6.3.2. Pro:
      Long-distance sex toys [543] already exist, and are popular; sex robots could be a welcome addition to this technological repertoire.

        1.6.3.2.1. Pro:
        People in long-distance relationships may be the target audience [544] for long range sex toys. As these relationships can be incredibly difficult, anything that makes it easier for people to maintain their connection while apart would be a great help.

        1.6.3.2.2. Con:
        Long-distance sex toys are subject to a number of inherent technological issues [545], including data privacy, connection issues, software crashes and platform compatibility. Such issues are likely to be more pronounced with increasingly advanced systems.

    1.6.4. Pro:
    Sex robots could be programmed to depict a more realistic experience of sexual activity, whereby they train their users to satisfy the typical desires of a real partner.

      1.6.4.1. Con:
      Sex robots are unlikely to strictly follow particular behaviours as different consumers may have contrary desires.

        1.6.4.1.1. Con:
        While people do find different sexual behaviours more or less appealing, the differences are not as profound as is commonly assumed; research has shown that a number of sexual behaviours are equally appealing to different genders (p. 7 [546]).

        1.6.4.1.2. Pro:
        Even if some behaviours become mandatory due to legislation or industry standardisation, it is possible that users will develop and distribute [547] their own software to achieve their desires.

      1.6.4.2. Pro:
      Sex robots that more closely mimic real sexual activity could allow people to gain confidence in the mechanics of sex before they are sexually active with others.

        1.6.4.2.1. Pro:
        People who suffer from issues such as erectile dysfunction and premature ejaculation may feel more comfortable using a sex robot for sexual satisfaction, and in time, this may make them more confident with regards to sex.

          1.6.4.2.1.1. Con:
          Sexual performance problems often stem from psychological distress [548]. As such, interactions with robots are unlikely to affect issues that spring from nervousness around other humans.

          1.6.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Sex therapists have indicated that sex robots could be used to support [549] those experiencing erectile dysfunction.

          1.6.4.2.1.3. Pro:
          People, particularly men, are increasingly [550] having sex later in life; sex therapists can support them in therapy through the use of surrogates, making sex robots potentially useful.

          1.6.4.2.1.4. Con:
          While sex robots could help address confidence issues in the bedroom, they are unlikely to help with any physiological causes, as they are not medical devices.

        1.6.4.2.2. Con:
        Desires can be situational and change depending on a variety of personal factors, making it difficult to universally train users to satisfy the wants of others.

        1.6.4.2.3. Pro:
        At least one sex robot user [551] claims that using sex robots has improved his performance in bed; such teaching may help remediate the orgasm gap [552].

      1.6.4.3. Pro:
      Sex robots may help people build a sense of comfort with sex, giving them more confidence.

        1.6.4.3.1. Con:
        Robots are unlikely to be able to measure whether an action is pleasurable (p. 7 [553]), inhibiting their usefulness as training tools for sexual experiences with human partners.

        1.6.4.3.2. Pro:
        Sex chatbots [554] can train people to flirt. Sex robots could assimilate this technology to allow for reciprocal interactions that support user education.

    1.6.5. Con:
    If the users of sex robots are disproportionately men, their experiences, expectations, and norms surrounding sex could change, becoming more divergent from those of women.

      1.6.5.1. Con:
      The sex toy industry has primarily addressed women [555], yet this has not created a discernible disjunct in how different genders experience sexuality.

        1.6.5.1.1. Pro:
        Disjuncts in the way that different genders experience sexuality are due to deep-seated cultural beliefs about sex and gender (p. 1 [556]) and are thus unlikely to be affected by sex toys or robots.

      1.6.5.2. Con:
      The character of changes to men's sexual norms and expectations would depend on the nature of the technology employed. Sex robots could have positive effects on male users and their expectations, benefitting society.

        1.6.5.2.1. Pro:
        Sex robot design can incorporate lessons from feminist pornography by focusing on improving the content, processes and contexts of the sex robot experience in a way that supports healthy expectations (pp. 13-14 [557]).

        1.6.5.2.2. Con:
        -> See 1.4.2.2.

      1.6.5.3. Pro:
      Many men [558], when surveyed, said they would have sex with robots, showing a clear gendered distinction in sexual preferences compared to women.

        1.6.5.3.1. Pro:
        Masculine sex robots are predominantly [559] ordered by men. This indicates that regardless of what a sex robot looks like, the main customer base is likely to be men.

          1.6.5.3.1.1. Pro:
          Men experience less stigma [560] than women for engaging in masturbatory activities as part of their sexuality which may influence sex robot user demographics.

            1.6.5.3.1.1.1. Pro:
            There is a huge media disjunct [561] in how male and female masturbation is portrayed and discussed.

            1.6.5.3.1.1.2. Con:
            A study suggested that only a minority of women experience shame and guilt around masturbation (p. 14 [562]).

          1.6.5.3.1.2. Con:
          The gender of those who purchase sex robots does not necessarily correlate with use of them; sex robots can be purchased [563] for use [564] by a couple (p. 10 [565]).

        1.6.5.3.2. Pro:
        Another study [566] found that over two-thirds of men would consider having sex with a robot, compared to just 37% of women.

        1.6.5.3.3. Con:
        As they often lead [567] the charge on other technological developments, women may rapidly take over men as early adopters of sex robot technology, provided that the gendered stigma of using sex robots is reduced.

        1.6.5.3.4. Con:
        The current understanding [568] of sex robot demographics seems to rest on an implicit assumption that women are uninterested in the technology. This underresearched assumption may yet prove to be false.

Sources:
[1] https://www.tht.org.uk/news/its-time-face-taboo-around-older-people-having-sex
"Despite this trend, the sexual activity of older people remains taboo in many areas of society. There is a tendency to desexualise people once they reach a certain age, resulting in a reluctance among many health professionals to openly discuss sexual health with older patients. We cannot separate our sexual health from our physical or mental health. Not only does this influence potential health outcomes, but it can also impact self-esteem, confidence and how relationships are perceived. Ultimately, this may make people avoid seeking help with problems related to their sexual health in the future."
[2] https://jme.bmj.com/content/medethics/47/1/26.full.pdf
"Losing the ability to express sexual feelings through the body deprives older people of an important source of love and meaning. To the extent that sexuality is entwined with a person’s identity, the erosion of sexual capability leads to a loss of one’s sense of gender identity, for example, ‘being a man’ or ‘being a woman’. When an older person’s body no longer does what they intend it to do, sex robots could intervene, enabling physical contact according to user specification. Just as service robots are being designed to assist older individuals with functions such as eating, dressing and bathing, they might be designed to assist with social functions, serving as sources of affiliation and sexual partnership."

However, it is not clear to what extent today’s social robots can lessen the load for caregivers. The effectiveness of companion robots will improve in time, but there is work to do."

[6] https://www.irt.org.au/the-good-life/how-to-help-the-elderly-cope-with-grief-and-loss/
"Keep in regular contact
Whether your loved one lives in their own home or in a residential aged care centre, it’s important to keep in regular contact and try visit them on a regular basis. If visiting isn’t possible, the next best thing is a phone call or video call to show you care. Your visits or calls will give your loved ones something to look forward to and can have a positive impact on their mental health. When you visit or call, ask how they’re feeling. Each day can be different for someone who is grieving – they might have been fine one day, but struggling the next. Take the time to listen and understand what they’re going through.

Listen – and let them express their grief
It’s important to let your loved one know it’s okay to grieve and mourn. At times like this, there’s not a lot you can say to make things better. Sometimes the best thing to do is to just be there and listen to what they have to say."
[7] https://www.bps.org.uk/psychologist/how-relationships-help-us-age-well
"As people live longer, there is an increasing interest in understanding and promoting health in later life. We hold that social relationships play a key role in maintaining older adults’ health, likely through the opportunities they provide for support exchanges, social engagement and sense of worth. This is recognised by older adults themselves, who endorse social relationships and social activities as being important for ageing well. A clearer understanding of the meaning of social relationships to older adults and the ways in which we maintain our social network as we age is vital to promoting successful ageing and promoting health in later life."

"Loneliness is a huge issue for the elderly, and sexual desire doesn’t just go out like the gas pilot light when you reach 70. I am still interested in sex, just as I was when I was 30, 40 and 50. There has been a rise in the divorce rate, leaving more middle aged (and older) people living alone, of which 2.21 million are over 70. These silver splitters might have got rid of the boring partner once the kids completed their education, but the prospect of sex-free decades in your seventies and eighties is really grim."
[9] https://www.psychologytoday.com/blog/all-about-sex/201003/do-older-adults-continue-have-sex
"Women over 40 start to experience menopausal changes: Vaginal lubrication declines. Intercourse may become uncomfortable. And libido and sexual self-esteem frequently decrease. Men over 40 start to notice that arousal takes more time. Erections become balky: slower to rise, less firm, and increasingly prone to wilting due to minor distractions. Finally, both men and women become more likely to have medical conditions and take medications that have sex-impairing side effects."
[10] https://www.researchgate.net/publication/14163926_Sexual_Activity_and_Satisfaction_Among_Very_Old_Adults_Results_From_a_Community-Dwelling_Medicare_Population_Survey
"Over two thirds reported that they were "very satisfied" or "satisfied" with their level of sexual activity or lack of sexual activity.
...
Women were more highly represented in the sexually inactive group, with almost twice as many inactive as active (66% vs 37%). However, there were significantly more women in the satisfied than in the dissatisfied group (61% vs 34%). Of the 625 women who responded to the satisfaction question, only 66 reported being dissatisfied or very dissatisfied with their sexual activity/inactivity. People in lower income groups were less likely to be active, but income made no difference in satisfaction. Finally, self-reported health and worry about health were both associated with sexual activity and satisfaction, and satisfaction is related to sexual activity. Of those who were active, fully 83% were satisfied or very satisfied compared with only 59% of those who were not active."

[12] https://slate.com/technology/2020/07/seniors-technology-illiteracy-misconception-pandemic.html
"Bran Knowles, a researcher who focuses on the intersection between data systems and social responsibility, studies how older adults use technology and why they do or don’t use certain devices or platforms. Her findings that tech use is about choice reflects Devitt and Stephens’ experiences. Often, the barrier is “a misalignment of values and products,” said Knowles—tech companies “don’t see [older people] as valid stakeholders.” That’s evident in how they fail to consider seniors’ needs, even when manufacturing products like the Jitterbug, a phone with extra-big buttons intended for older users. However, button size doesn’t dictate seniors’ decisions about tech use, and such presumptions highlight Silicon Valley’s bias toward youth, both in terms of shareholders and user markets. The marketing of Jitterbug seems geared toward the children or caretakers of seniors, rather than toward seniors themselves, and user reviews similarly tend to be from those children and caretakers."
[13] https://www.cnet.com/news/robot-seal-paro-comforts-japan-tsunami-victims/
"Modeled on a baby harp seal, Paro is a therapeutic robot that responds to touch and voices. It's covered in tactile sensors and responds to petting by squealing. It's meant to soothe people who use it.

Developed by Takanori Shibata at Japan's National Institute of Advanced Industrial Science and Technology (AIST), Paro has been used in nursing homes in Japan and overseas since 2003; eight generations have been produced. Studies have shown that Paro can lower stress levels in users and caregivers. It can also help dementia patients."

[17] https://www.lgbthero.org.uk/loneliness-and-being-lgbtq-2
"LGBTQ+ people are more likely to be lonely than cis-het people. This loneliness can stem from a variety of factors: we’re more likely to be disconnected from our families, or have problems finding other people like us who we can connect with.. 

LGBTQ+ people are also more likely to face discrimination due to their sexual orientation and/or gender identity, which can lead to feelings of isolation and difficulties getting close to other people.

Older LGBTQ+ people may experience heightened loneliness and feelings of disconnection from a queer community that’s increasingly centered around young folks. That is, older queer folks may no longer want to go to LGBTQ+ clubs, bars, pubs, etc., but find themselves unable to find a replacement community where they can connect with their peers."

This may be due to the fact that more conservative sex education programs, such as abstinence-only programs, often do not include as much concrete and comprehensive knowledge about sex, such as human anatomy and other methods of birth control, rather than strictly abstinence."

You can call the LGBT+ switchboard to find out about socials and gatherings; the volunteer group operates from cities across the country and is the oldest information and advice point, providing callers with anything from local listings to a friendly ear. Harm reduction is grounded in justice and human rights. It focuses on positive change and on working with people without judgement, coercion, discrimination, or requiring that they stop using drugs as a precondition of support."

[37] https://www.womensrepublic.net/lets-talk-about-bioessentialism-and-transphobia/
"Bioessentialism, a shorthand for biological essentialism, is the idea that we are born with specific, immutable traits by virtue of our sex. To a bioessentialist, sex maketh man. “Sex,” in this case, means everything from hormones like testosterone to “manly” traits such as not displaying emotion. This line of reasoning formed the cornerstone of traditional patriarchal structures: “Men are stronger, less emotional and better suited to lead.” It persists today in the form of gender roles, gender-based exclusion, and transphobia."
[38] https://web.archive.org/web/20210109111757id_/https://jme.bmj.com/content/medethics/47/1/26.full.pdf
"Whether age-related changes or disease and disability result in older people being deprived of sexual capabilities depends on the supportive services available. With assistance, older adults can continue to be sexual in ways they value, including sustaining sexually intimate relationships, deriving pleasure from sexual activity and preserving high-quality sexual lives. Evidence indicates that older adults want resources to gain greater control and autonomous choice over their sexual lives and want to improve their ability and desire to give and receive pleasure. Sex robots represent one important tool to help older people with disability maintain sexual health."

[57] https://kidshealth.org/en/teens/curable.html
"The term "cure" means that, after medical treatment, the patient no longer has that particular condition anymore.

Some diseases can be cured. Others, like hepatitis B, have no cure. The person will always have the condition, but medical treatments can help to manage the disease...

When a disease can't be cured, doctors often use treatments to help control it. For example, one type of diabetes happens when the pancreas does not make enough insulin to get glucose into cells where it's needed. Doctors treat people with diabetes using insulin injections and other methods so they can continue to live normal lives. But right now there's no cure for diabetes. So some people need insulin treatments for the rest of their lives."
[58] https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/consent-to-treatment/
"For consent to be valid, it must be voluntary and informed, and the person consenting must have the capacity to make the decision.

The meaning of these terms are:

 voluntary – the decision to either consent or not to consent to treatment must be made by the person, and must not be influenced by pressure from medical staff, friends or family
 informed – the person must be given all of the information about what the treatment involves, including the benefits and risks, whether there are reasonable alternative treatments, and what will happen if treatment does not go ahead
 capacity – the person must be capable of giving consent, which means they understand the information given to them and can use it to make an informed decision"

[63] http://www.bbc.com/future/story/20130901-is-the-uncanny-valley-real
"From humanoid robot heads to super-realistic prosthetic hands, the uncanny valley is where robots that give us the creeps live. It is the range between obvious cartoons and discernibly real people, where things look almost lifelike, and yet not quite believable. Peering into the uncanny valley is an uncomfortable experience. Its residents, like Kaspar, have a way of eliciting feelings of disgust, fear or dread.

[...] But now the concept itself is coming under scrutiny like never before. What exactly we are feeling and why we feel this way are questions that have finally found their way under the microscope. And some researchers are asking whether the uncanny valley exists at all."
[64] https://www.simplypsychology.org/uncanny-valley.html#Criticism
"Several objections have been raised that challenge the scientific semblance of the uncanny valley theory.

First, the uncanny valley may comprise manifold heterogenous phenomena stemming from multiple and overlapping causes. These may involve learned or evolved circuits for face perception.

Moreover, the role played by distinct sense modalities and psychological constructs cannot be discounted. For instance, an observer’s cultural background may significantly influence the perception of an android and, therefore, the dynamics of the valley in a particular situation.

This suggests that younger people who are more familiar with robots and androids would be less likely to be impacted by the hypothesized uncanny effect.

A second criticism holds that the uncanny valley is merely a result of humans’ greater familiarity with fellow humans and that it does not indicate a unique phenomenon."

"Mori placed a humanoid robot right before the dip into the uncanny valley, and his theory focused on the design of humanlike robots and user experience in regards to the robots’ appearance and behaviors. RAPT predicts that exposure to robots over time will reduce aspects of uncanniness, even in humanlike robots that are recognizable as artificial life. The Uncanny Valley theory illustrates the idea of repulsion felt by people encountering some classes of humanlike robots, while RAPT acknowledges this uncanniness, but regards it as a temporary condition, open to change at individual and cultural levels. Robot sex workers can then take on many types of humanlike design, not just because of formal design processes, changing standards of attractiveness, customization, or jury-rigging, but because people accept robots in general as potentially sexual in some contexts. In that way, even less humanlike robots will eventually become acceptable RSWs."

An empathetic robot has a heart, and that heart is a piece of software called the empathy module. An empathy module analyzes facial cues, acoustic markers in speech and the content of speech itself to read human emotion and tell the robot how to respond."

[138] https://www.brookings.edu/techstream/the-danger-of-anthropomorphic-language-in-robotic-ai-systems/
"When describing the behavior of robotic systems, we tend to rely on anthropomorphisms. Cameras “see,” decision algorithms “think,” and classification systems “recognize.” But the use of such terms can set us up for failure, since they create expectations and assumptions that often do not hold, especially in the minds of people who have no training in the underlying technologies involved. This is particularly problematic because many of the tasks we envision for robotic technologies are typically ones that humans currently do (or could do) some part of. The natural tendency is to describe these tasks as a human would using the “skills” a human has—which may be very different from how a robot performs the task."
[139] https://www.datarobot.com/blog/humans-and-ai-should-we-describe-ai-as-autonomous/
"To get from machine learning to an AI system, you need to broaden step four to convert machine learning predictions into decisions (for example, to offer product X to customer Y or to flag a transaction as fraudulent).

At no point in this process does the AI system get to choose its own goals or make decisions without human governance. The AI system is just a computer system, a tool to be used by humans. It is designed by humans, built by humans, managed by humans, with the objective to serve human goals. You don’t have to negotiate with an AI system to get it to do its job. AI systems are autonomous to the extent that they can make individual decisions without direct human interference in the moment, and, critically, without human capability to intercede, to interrupt, or to modify.

The dictionary definition of automated is “operated by largely automatic equipment.”

If we ignore the hype, your AI system is automated, not autonomous."

Consent involves changing the balance of those rights and corresponding obligations. By consenting to having sex with Jack, Jill temporarily waives her right to physical inviolability. She relieves Jack of his obligation not to cross the boundaries of her sexual autonomy, and in most instances, simultaneously relieves the state of its obligation to protect her from Jack's boundary-crossing conduct. Moreover, by consenting to having sex with Jack, Jill not only relieves the state of its obligation to protect Jill - Jill demands that the state not intervene (e.g., by punishing Jack) in this consensual transaction because such intervention would violate both Jack's and Jill's autonomy."

In most of geography's history, subjectivity has meant understanding the role of various social locations (such as class, gender, ethnicity, and sexuality) on the construction of the individual's relationship with the world, which shapes their knowledge and understanding of the world. This suggests that there will always be a bias to this perception and the knowledge that emerges from it. Besides having profound epistemological and ontological implications, this has a significant effect on the development of research methodology: The subject's experiences and biography influence their knowledge and understanding of the world."

[170] https://www.regulation.org.uk/deregulation-regulatory_budgets.html
"Regulatory costs may in principle divided into policy costs, one-off costs, financial costs and administrative costs.

Policy costs are those costs directly attributable to the policy goal...
One-off costs are those costs only sustained once (such as in taking professional advice and initial staff training) when a business is adapting to a new or amended regulation.
Financial costs include paying tax and license fees.
Administrative costs are all those costs associated with familiarisation, record keeping and reporting, including inspection and enforcement. Such costs would include ongoing staff training, keeping records of exposure levels and equipment checks, and supplying that information to the authorities."

"The arrival of the internet caused a large decline in both the pecuniary and non-pecuniary costs of accessing pornography. Using state-level panel data from 1998-2003, I find that the arrival of the internet was associated with a reduction in rape incidence. While the internet is obviously used for many purposes other than pornography, it is notable that growth in internet usage had no apparent effect on other crimes. Moreover, when I disaggregate the rape data by offender age, I find that the effect of the internet on rape is concentrated among those for whom the internet-induced fall in the non-pecuniary price of pornography was the largest – men ages 15-19, who typically live with their parents. These results, which suggest that pornography and rape are substitutes, are in contrast with previous laboratory studies, most of which do not allow for potential substitutability between pornography and rape."

Anxiety plays an important role in the pathogenesis and maintenance of SDs. This co-presence is very common in clinical practice: patients with SDs will often present with an anxiety disorder, and in many cases it is unclear which is the primary disorder. On the other hand, for many patients with a psychiatric disorder an SD may be a persistent disturbance.

"Outercourse activities are 100% effective at preventing pregnancy: kissing, massage, masturbation, dry humping with clothes on, anal sex, and oral sex can’t cause pregnancy.

That said, pregnancy can happen, rarely, even if you don’t actually have vaginal sex. If semen (cum) accidentally gets on a vulva, sperm can swim up into the vagina and fertilize an egg.

There are a few ways this can happen:

Cum gets on the vulva. It can accidentally drip or be wiped onto the vulva.

There’s wet semen on someone’s fingers and/or sex toys, and they touch the vulva or go in the vagina.

Semen drips out of the anus and into the vulva after anal sex.

Bottom line: if you’re trying to avoid pregnancy, don’t get semen (cum) on the vulva or in the vagina. Pre-ejaculate (pre-cum) can also have some sperm in it, so keep pre-cum away from the vulva and vagina, too."
[254] https://www.emedicinehealth.com/ask_is_birth_control_100_percent_effective/article_em.htm
"Birth control pills are one of the most effective forms of contraception, but they are not 100 percent effective. When taken exactly as prescribed, birth control pills can technically be 99% effective in preventing pregnancy. But in real life, it is often difficult to take the pills exactly as prescribed and so the overall, real-life effectiveness is about 91%. Abstinence is the only 100% effective method of preventing pregnancy.

Intrauterine devices (IUDs), sterilization (tubal ligation for women or vasectomy for men), and birth control implants are about 99% effective in preventing pregnancy. The birth control shot is about 94% effective.

Birth control patches and the vaginal ring are equally as effective as birth control pills, preventing pregnancy 91% of the time."

Researchers at the Sapienza University of Rome, Italy, studied mitochondrial DNA (inherited from mothers) and genetic markers on the Y chromosome (inherited from fathers) in 40 populations from sub-Saharan Africa. "With any new technology – especially electronic goods (TVs, computers, cameras), we tend to see a sharp fall in price over time. Initially, the new technology is very expensive, and a high percentage of disposable income, but over time, prices tend to fall considerably. There are a number of reasons to explain this fall in price.

-Quantum improvements in technology, reducing costs of production.

-Economies of scale from increasing production

-Increased competition as more firms enters the market, driving down prices.

-An element of price skimming. Initial high prices to take advantage of customers with inelastic demand."

"We frequently make poor choices that we know we will regret. The same is true with deciding whether to use technology. While we can and do refuse to use it, we do not necessarily make good choices about how and when to use it.

Technology is neutral in the sense that it merely provides options; it does not compel anyone to take up those options. Different people, and by extension different cultures, will make different choices of which uses to make of technology. However, technology is directional in that it allows a culture to move in a direction that it would otherwise have been unable to do. For technology to change a culture, the people in that culture must choose to use it. That choice may be made poorly or wisely, but the choice is the key factor."

[397] https://www.britannica.com/topic/the-personal-is-political
"Although the origin of the phrase “the personal is political” is uncertain, it became popular following the publication in 1970 of an essay of the same name by American feminist Carol Hanisch, who argued that many personal experiences (particularly those of women) can be traced to one’s location within a system of power relationships. 
...
The statement that “the personal is political” was influential in second-wave feminism, shaping the development of social analyses and theories, encouraging new types of activism, and widening the scope of issues that could be defined as “feminist issues.” For example, it was one of the premises underlying the creation of feminist consciousness-raising groups, which met to discuss topics such as careers or parenting, sharing their personal experiences and generating new knowledge based on those experiences. This knowledge was the basis for further activism."

Feminists asserted that single women had the same sexual desires and should have the same sexual freedoms as everyone else in society. For feminists, the sexual revolution was about female sexual empowerment. For social conservatives, the sexual revolution was an invitation for promiscuity and an attack on the very foundation of American society — the family. Feminists and social conservatives quickly clashed over morality of the "sexual revolution," and the Pill was drawn into the debate."

[412] https://asiasociety.org/education/shinto
"Shinto (literally “the way of the gods”) is Japan's native belief system and predates historical records. The many practices, attitudes, and institutions that have developed to make up Shinto revolve around the Japanese land and seasons and their relation with the human inhabitants. Expressions of Shinto beliefs toward nature include the recognition of a divine spirit (kami) in venerable old trees, large mountains, and tall waterfalls, as well as celebrations of the highlights of each season. (This reverence is often expressed by the placement of a small shrine next to the natural element being celebrated or garlanding it with a white rope.) Traditionally, Shinto also involves purification rites and customs to overcome the polluting effects of death and decay. However, Shinto does not espouse a moral code, lacks religious scriptures, and does not conceive of a life after death."

"Overall, the researchers found, intraspecies killing was the cause of about 0.3 percent of mammal deaths.

But for the ancestor of all primates, rodents and hares, killings caused about 1.1 percent of deaths, rising to 2.3 percent for the next, more recent, common ancestor of primates and tree shrews.

By the time the common human ancestor first appeared around 200,000-160,000 years ago, the rate was about two percent—similar to that for other primates, the team found.

"This means that humans have phylogenetically inherited their propensity for violence," they wrote."

[529] https://www.mindful.org/empathy-fatigue-is-all-too-real/
"Call it what you like: Habituation. Outrage exhaustion. Desensitization. Compassion fatigue (a now-popular term; some experts prefer empathy fatigue). All describe how the emotions that the brain generated the first time we witnessed, even at a remove, something that incited anger or heartache or shock become exhausted in the face of repeated tragedy or horror, with the result that the tenth, or hundredth, repetition provokes a mere shadow of the initial reaction.
...
To be clear, emotional desensitization isn’t always maladaptive. The effect is so strong that it is the basis for a widely practiced form of psychological intervention: exposure therapy. Used to help people overcome obsessive-compulsive disorder, phobias, post-traumatic stress, and other conditions rooted in anxiety, the therapy involves giving the patient increasing “exposures” to whatever memories, experiences, or other triggers set off the disorder."

For the relationship to be secure, or to become securely attached, that response system needs to be mutual.

Codependence, on the other hand, operates in a unidirectional way, with the codependent partner meeting the needs of their partner, without this being reciprocated."

This picture of UL highlights its paradoxical nature. [556] https://www.oecd.org/dac/gender-development/1850708.pdf
"Gender (like race or ethnicity) functions as an organizing principle for society because of the cultural meanings given to being male or female. This is evident in the division of labour according to gender. In most societies there are clear patterns of “women’s work” and “men’s work,” both in the household and in the wider community – and cultural explanations of why this should be so. The patterns and the explanations differ among societies and change over time. While the specific nature of gender relations varies among societies, the general pattern is that women have less personal autonomy, fewer resources at their disposal, and limited influence over the decision-making processes that shape their societies and their own lives. This pattern of disparity based on gender is both a human rights and a development issue."
